# GTM and Partners

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 720px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 179px;"><col style="width: 185px;"><col style="width: 187px;"><col style="width: 169px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="179"><p><strong>Who are we selling to?</strong></p><p>Tier-1 MNOs and government ICT ministries in non-U.S.-aligned markets. The primary route is B2B2C — selling sovereign-grade LEO capacity wholesale to MNOs who distribute it to consumers and enterprises under their own brands.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="185"><p><strong>How do we enter?</strong></p><p>Phased across four beachheads: South Korea (internal pilot, months 1–18), India (primary scale engine, months 19–36), Indonesia (archipelagic proof point) and Turkey (MENA anchor), each gate-controlled by signed LOIs and regulatory access.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="187"><p><strong>What partnerships enable it?</strong></p><p>A diversified launch coalition (Ariane 6, ULA Vulcan, Blue Origin, ISRO) to avoid SpaceX dependency, plus joint-venture MNO relationships in each beachhead for spectrum access, domestic gateway hosting, and last-mile distribution.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="169"><p><strong>What validates the go decision?</strong></p><p>Three commercial commitments before Phase 2 capital is released: binding LOIs from ≥2 MNOs, confirmed ≥50% MNO gateway co-investment, and launch capacity secured at &lt;$2,000/kg from non-SpaceX providers.</p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

## Executive Summary

This chapter defines Samsung's go-to-market strategy for a sovereign-aligned, B2B2C LEO satellite broadband constellation targeting non-U.S.-aligned markets in South/Southeast Asia and the Middle East. The strategy covers customer targeting, beachhead sequencing, partnership architecture, phased execution, regulatory navigation, and the 18-month validation programme required before full constellation capital is committed. The core commercial premise is that Samsung does not need to beat Starlink at broadband — it needs to be the only credible non-U.S., non-Chinese provider willing to route traffic through domestic gateways, and to offer that through MNO channels that governments already trust.

## Key Takeaways

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 809px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 90px;"><col style="width: 719px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p>&nbsp;</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="719"><p><strong>Takeaway</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>1</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="719"><p><strong>Bypass D2C entirely — the B2B2C wholesale model is the only viable entry path.</strong></p><p>Starlink and Kuiper already dominate the direct-to-consumer broadband segment with massive capital, established infrastructure, and aggressive pricing. Samsung's competitive window exists exclusively in the B2B2C wholesale channel, where MNO trust, sovereign compliance, and device integration are valued above raw price. Attempting a D2C retail play would position Samsung as a late, under-capitalised Starlink clone.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>2</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="719"><p><strong>South Korea is a testbed, not a market — the real revenue gates are India and Indonesia.</strong></p><p>South Korea's high terrestrial penetration means it offers no commercial demand for satellite broadband. Its value is purely operational: a controlled environment to validate gateway architecture, device NTN handoff, and billing integration with domestic telcos before capital is deployed in high-friction emerging markets. Treating it as a revenue market would distort the business case.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>3</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="719"><p><strong>The launch coalition is the single most fragile dependency in the entire GTM plan.</strong></p><p>Every phase-gate and revenue forecast assumes the availability of non-SpaceX, non-Chinese heavy-lift capacity at competitive pricing. Current market evidence shows Ariane 6 delayed, ULA Vulcan grounded for national security reviews, and Blue Origin TeraWave competing for the same manifest slots Samsung needs. The GTM plan must treat launch capacity as a strategic procurement problem requiring binding take-or-pay contracts, not a logistics detail.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>4</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="719"><p><strong>MNO channel lock-out is the fastest path to commercial failure — move before Starlink closes it.</strong></p><p>Starlink is already executing direct-to-cell partnerships with T-Mobile, Optus, KDDI, Bharti Airtel (India), and Globe Telecom (Philippines). AST SpaceMobile has signed with Vodafone and Verizon. Every MNO that signs an exclusive with an incumbent is a blocked distribution channel and a lost spectrum access route. Samsung's most time-sensitive commercial action is to secure conditional MoUs with anchor MNOs in India, Indonesia, and Turkey before those operators commit to competitors.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>5</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="719"><p><strong>The Galaxy ecosystem converts the terminal cost problem into a structural moat — if NTN integration is prioritised now.</strong></p><p>Starlink's $599 user terminal is its primary consumer adoption barrier in price-sensitive markets. Samsung's Exynos NTN modem and Galaxy smartphone footprint (~200M devices/year) can eliminate this barrier entirely by making existing handsets the user terminal. This advantage is time-sensitive: Qualcomm and MediaTek are embedding NTN into standard Android chipsets, and once they achieve parity, Samsung's device moat erodes. The NTN integration roadmap must be treated as a commercial priority, not a product feature.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>6</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="719"><p><strong>Pricing must be structured as wholesale capacity, not consumer subscriptions — at least through Phase 2.</strong></p><p>Selling bandwidth in bulk to MNO partners at a per-Gbps wholesale rate and allowing them to set retail prices is the only model that avoids direct competition with Starlink's $120/month consumer offering while Samsung is still in the cost-ramp phase. Direct consumer pricing before terminal costs fall below $200 and before SLA-grade network density is achieved would generate churn, damage the brand, and give regulators a pricing reference they can use against Samsung in subsidy negotiations.</p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

## Target Customers & Beachhead Markets

Samsung's GTM must bypass the direct-to-consumer broadband segment dominated by SpaceX and Amazon and focus exclusively on the B2B2C wholesale channel, the B2G government security channel, and the device-native D2D consumer channel. The entry sequence across four geographies is designed to control capital exposure while generating the commercial proof points required to unlock Phase 2 constellation capital.

### Customer Proof Points Required for Commercial Commitment

The buying journey for MNOs and government ministries involves 12–24-month procurement cycles driven by spectrum licensing approvals, national security audits, and infrastructure planning. Before any MNO will sign a binding LOI, Samsung must deliver three specific proof points:

External legal audit in 3 beachhead markets confirming LI compliance.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p>Core Network Integration</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="425"><p>The satellite network integrates seamlessly with existing MNO terrestrial core networks via open APIs, enabling unified customer billing, uninterrupted handoff, and SD-WAN failover without bespoke integration work per operator.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="241"><p>Phase 1 South Korea pilot with KT Corp/SK Telecom on live network. ## Partnership Architecture

Samsung's GTM depends on two distinct partnership classes that must be secured in sequence. The launch coalition removes the most critical supply-chain bottleneck — the absence of a proprietary launch vehicle. The MNO joint ventures provide the market access, spectrum, and last-mile distribution that makes the business commercially viable. Both must be structured as binding, long-term agreements rather than exploratory MoUs.

### Launch Coalition Strategy

Operating a multi-tens-of-billions satellite business without a proprietary launch vehicle is feasible but requires a structured multi-provider procurement strategy. Samsung must form a binding 'Launch Coalition' of non-SpaceX, non-Chinese heavy-lift providers, using multi-year take-or-pay contracts across the coalition to maintain acceptable cost and schedule predictability for a 3,500-satellite deployment.

Pentagon track record reduces regulatory friction for dual-use payloads.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>&nbsp;High — timing</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p>Blue Origin</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p>New Glenn</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="304"><p>45,000 kg LEO payload. Note: Blue Origin's TeraWave constellation competes for the same launch windows Samsung requires.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="286"><p>Highest payload capacity. Conflict-of-interest risk must be managed contractually — separate NewSpace and commercial launch arms.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>&nbsp;High — CoI</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p>ISRO / NewSpace India Ltd.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p>LVM3 / GSLV Mk III</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="304"><p>10,000 kg LEO payload. Proven constellation experience (OneWeb). India partnership creates co-investment incentive for Indian market access.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="286"><p>Beachhead-aligned provider. Combining launch contracts with Samsung's India MNO JV negotiations strengthens government relations.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>&nbsp;Low</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p>Mitsubishi Heavy Industries</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p>H3</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="304"><p>6,500 kg LEO payload. Operational 2024. Japanese government-backed — strategic alignment with Samsung's Korean sovereign positioning.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="286"><p>Supplementary provider for smaller batch deployments and regional orbit insertion.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>&nbsp;Low</strong></p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 720px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 720px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="720"><p><strong>Coalition Procurement Principle</strong></p><p>Samsung must secure take-or-pay contracts with a minimum of three coalition providers before committing Phase 2 constellation capital. No single provider should represent more than 40% of annual launch cadence. Launch contracts must include force majeure carve-outs specific to national security restrictions and regulatory grounding events.</p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

### MNO Partnership Structure by Beachhead

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 932px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 106px;"><col style="width: 126px;"><col style="width: 348px;"><col style="width: 186px;"><col style="width: 166px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="106"><p><strong>Market</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="126"><p><strong>Target MNO(s)</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="348"><p><strong>Partnership Rationale</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="186"><p><strong>Samsung Contribution</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="166"><p><strong>MNO Contribution</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="106"><p><strong>India</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="126"><p>Bharti Airtel (primary), Reliance Jio (secondary)</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="348"><p>Airtel has existing satellite internet interests (OneWeb stake) but no exclusive LEO partnership. XL Axiata's archipelago coverage infrastructure provides ready-made backhaul integration points for satellite nodes.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="186"><p>Sovereign LEO backhaul, low-cost terminals via Samsung Electronics Indonesia, direct MNO API integration.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="166"><p>USO anchor contract sponsorship, spectrum filing, 17,000-island distribution infrastructure.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="106"><p><strong>Turkey</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="126"><p>Türk Telekom, Turkcell</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="348"><p>Both MNOs have government shareholder stakes — alignment with Ankara's sovereignty objectives is commercially guaranteed. Turkey's position as a NATO member that refuses Starlink exclusivity makes Samsung's neutral positioning uniquely attractive.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="186"><p>Sovereignty architecture, Samsung Networks 5G integration continuity, gateway technology.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="166"><p>MENA regulatory access, landing rights, Turkish spectrum filing, enterprise B2B channel access.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="106"><p><strong>South Korea</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="126"><p>KT Corp, SK Telecom</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="348"><p>Pilot validation only — no commercial revenue target. Relationship depth allows rapid protocol integration and honest performance feedback before markets with higher regulatory stakes.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="186"><p>Pilot hardware, full engineering team access, preferred vendor pricing on 5G equipment.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="166"><p>Live network test access, regulatory sandbox environment, honest performance feedback.</p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

### Value Exchange Framework

The structure of MNO relationships must shift away from standard vendor-client contracts toward Joint Ventures or deep revenue-sharing agreements. This model directly counters Starlink's carrier partnerships, which are structured as service agreements that keep the MNO as a reseller rather than a co-investor.

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 720px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 219px;"><col style="width: 238px;"><col style="width: 263px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="219"><p><strong>Samsung Provides</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="238"><p><strong>MNO Provides</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="263"><p><strong>Shared Outcome</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="219"><p><strong>Infrastructure: </strong>Sovereign-neutral satellite capacity routed through local gateways with LI compliance by design</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Hardware: </strong>Low-cost user terminals (target &lt;$200 by Phase 3) and Galaxy NTN device integration</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Technology: </strong>Open MNO API integration for unified billing, SD-WAN failover, and 5G core handoff</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Relationships: </strong>5G equipment supply continuity, Samsung Networks enterprise sales support</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="238"><p><strong>Spectrum &amp; Access: </strong>National spectrum licences, landing rights, regulatory sponsorship with local telecom authorities</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Infrastructure: </strong>≥50% gateway CapEx co-investment, backhaul siting rights, ground station real estate</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Distribution: </strong>Existing consumer/enterprise billing relationships, retail channel, rural coverage infrastructure</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Trust: </strong>Government and regulator relationships that Samsung cannot acquire as a foreign entrant</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="263"><p><strong>Revenue: </strong>Wholesale capacity revenue for Samsung; incremental ARPU from new coverage areas for MNO</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Compliance: </strong>USO mandate fulfilment for MNO; sovereign infrastructure credentials for Samsung</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Competitive moat: </strong>Joint market positioning against U.S. LEO incumbents — strengthens both parties' negotiating leverage with national regulators</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Optionality: </strong>JV structure gives both parties preferential access to 5G-Satellite converged network contracts as NTN standards mature</p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

## GTM Execution Plan

The execution plan sequences market entry to balance capital exposure against the need for rapid commercial validation. Each phase has explicit gate criteria that must be met before capital is committed to the next phase. No phase proceeds on the basis of targets — only on signed commitments.

Zero friction for users — no hardware purchase, no separate subscription. Samsung MX markets NTN as a Galaxy premium feature.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="256"><p>Galaxy smartphone users in low-terrestrial-coverage areas (~200M devices/year)</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>&nbsp;Phase 3</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="126"><p>Platform Integration (API-first)</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="467"><p>Open APIs allow MNOs to integrate Samsung satellite connectivity directly into their existing apps, billing stacks, and enterprise portals. Reduces onboarding friction and strengthens JV switching costs.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="256"><p>MNO IT teams, enterprise integrators, IoT platform operators</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>&nbsp;Phase 2–3</strong></p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

### Pricing Strategy

The pricing strategy prioritises market penetration and commercial validation over margin capture during Phases 1–2. Consumer retail pricing is explicitly deferred until Phase 3, when terminal costs and network density can support competitive positioning against Starlink.

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 822px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 166px;"><col style="width: 191px;"><col style="width: 196px;"><col style="width: 269px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="166"><p><strong>Tier</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="191"><p><strong>Samsung Price</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="196"><p><strong>Competitor Benchmark</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="269"><p><strong>Rationale</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="166"><p><strong>User Terminal (Hardware)</strong></p><p>Phase 1–2</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="191"><p>Target: &lt;$299 at scale (Phase 2). Phase 1 pilot terminals subsidised to MNO partners at cost.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="196"><p>Starlink: $599 standard terminal. Kuiper: expected ~$400 at launch.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="269"><p>Samsung Foundry vertical integration on phased-array antenna and Exynos modem drives BOM below competitor hardware costs. Galaxy NTN eliminates hardware cost entirely for D2D channel.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="166"><p><strong>Wholesale Capacity (B2B2C MNO)</strong></p><p>Phase 2–3</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="191"><p>Per-Gbps wholesale rate to MNO partners. Priced to allow MNO resale margin while undercutting MSP/SD-WAN OpEx by ≥30%.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="196"><p>Current MSP/SD-WAN workarounds: $10k–$1M/year per fleet cluster. GEO VSAT: $65+/Mbit/s/month.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="269"><p>Wholesale model avoids direct consumer price competition with Starlink ($120/month) while validating MNO economics. MNO sets retail price; Samsung captures capacity revenue.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="166"><p><strong>Government / B2G Sovereign Contracts</strong></p><p>Phase 2–3</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="191"><p>Premium pricing for dedicated gateway capacity, LI compliance services, and SLA-guaranteed uptime. Negotiated multi-year contract.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="196"><p>No direct published competitor rate — sovereign contracts are bespoke. Yahsat GCC contracts estimated $200M–$5B range.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="269"><p>Sovereignty premium is validated at the B2G level, not consumer level. Government buyers accept premium pricing for compliance and data residency guarantees.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="166"><p><strong>D2D Consumer (Galaxy)</strong></p><p>Phase 3 only</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="191"><p>Bundled with Galaxy premium device tier or low-cost add-on to existing mobile plan. No standalone hardware charge.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="196"><p>Starlink Direct-to-Cell: negotiated with carriers, not priced direct to consumer. Apple Emergency SOS: included in iPhone purchase.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="269"><p>Galaxy NTN tier launched only after Phase 2 orbital demonstration proves reliability. Positioned as a safety and coverage feature, not a broadband substitute.</p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

## Stakeholder & Regulatory Navigation

Successful market entry requires coordinating a complex network of internal sponsors and external gatekeepers. The engagement sequence — MNO JV structuring first, spectrum filing second, government anchor tenant third — is not arbitrary. It reflects the political reality that in every beachhead market, MNOs have more regulatory influence than foreign satellite operators. Samsung must use its MNO relationships as the vehicle for regulatory access, not a substitute for it.

Joint application with anchor MNO where possible. WRC-27 D2D spectrum decisions require filings to be in place by 2026.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="96"><p>Months 6–12</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>4</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p>National Security Compliance Certification</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="581"><p>Submit the 'gateway-in-a-box' architecture for formal national security audit in each beachhead market. External legal counsel from local space law specialists must confirm LI compliance before commercial launch in that market.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="96"><p>Months 10–18</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>5</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p>Enterprise Anchor Tenant Acquisition</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="581"><p>Target government and large enterprise contracts through the MNO's existing B2B sales channels. At this stage, the MNO's sovereign credentials and Samsung's compliance-by-design architecture are the combined sales proposition.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="96"><p>Months 12–24</p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

## Market Validation

The 18-month validation programme is designed to convert qualified demand signals into binding commercial commitments before Phase 2 constellation capital is authorised. It is not a research programme — it is a commercial execution sprint with hard binary decision gates at every milestone.

### 18-Month Validation Timeline

### Pivot & Scale Criteria

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 809px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 146px;"><col style="width: 285px;"><col style="width: 378px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p><strong>Decision</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="285"><p><strong>Trigger</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="378"><p><strong>Response</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="3" rowspan="1" colwidth="146,285,378"><p><strong>PROCEED</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p>Scale to Phase 2</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="285"><p>All four Phase 1 leading indicators met: ≥2 LOIs, ≥50% gateway co-investment confirmed, launch capacity secured, spectrum filed in ≥2 markets.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="378"><p>Authorise Phase 2 capital. Formal JV legal structuring with anchor MNOs. Begin procurement of 50–100 satellite constellation segment.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="3" rowspan="1" colwidth="146,285,378"><p><strong>PIVOT</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p>Launch Supply Failure</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="285"><p>Cannot secure minimum annual launch cadence from non-SpaceX providers by Month 6.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="378"><p>Pause constellation deployment. Pivot to a smaller regional constellation (500–1,000 satellites) or a GEO-LEO hybrid architecture that reduces launch cadence requirement. Re-scope to a single beachhead market.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p>MNO Rejection — No Co-Investment</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="285"><p>Target MNOs refuse gateway co-investment but confirm demand for sovereign LEO capacity.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="378"><p>Pivot to Samsung-funded OpEx model for gateway infrastructure. Revise financial model — extends breakeven by 2–3 years. Requires additional Phase 2 capital authorisation.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p>Sovereign Premium Too Low</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="285"><p>Conjoint analysis shows MNOs will not pay a premium sufficient to cover the non-vertically-integrated launch cost delta vs. SpaceX.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="378"><p>Pivot strategy to D2D-only: strip broadband from the constellation design and focus exclusively on narrowband IoT and D2D messaging through Galaxy. Dramatically reduces constellation size and capital requirement.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="3" rowspan="1" colwidth="146,285,378"><p><strong>KILL</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p>Pain Not Actionable</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="285"><p>Target MNOs acknowledge sovereignty risks of U.S. incumbents but refuse to switch — indicating the pain of the status quo does not exceed the adoption friction.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="378"><p>Fundamental demand thesis invalidated. Discontinue the venture. Redirect semiconductor and device NTN investment into existing Samsung Networks 5G product lines.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p>Compliance Blockage</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="285"><p>Formal ITAR/EAR legal review determines that export controls prevent service delivery to the primary beachhead markets.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="378"><p>Abandon the venture unless a fully non-U.S.-supply-chain alternative can be architected within 6 months.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p>MNO Channel Locked</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="285"><p>All three beachhead MNO targets (India, Indonesia, Turkey) sign exclusive agreements with Starlink, Kuiper, or AST before Samsung secures LOIs.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="378"><p>Abandon the B2B2C GTM model. Assess whether a pure B2G government sales model (without MNO distribution) is economically viable at reduced scale.</p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

## Key Assumptions

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 809px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 90px;"><col style="width: 340px;"><col style="width: 227px;"><col style="width: 152px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>#</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="340"><p><strong>Assumption</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="227"><p><strong>Confirms If…</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="152"><p><strong>Rejects If…</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>1</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="340"><p><strong>MNOs will pay a sovereign premium AND co-invest in domestic gateway CapEx.</strong></p><p>The entire B2B2C GTM model rests on MNOs both buying wholesale capacity at above-Starlink prices AND sharing gateway infrastructure costs.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="227"><p>≥2 conditional LOIs include a price per Gbps ≥15% above GEO VSAT rates; ≥2 MNOs sign 50/50 gateway CapEx term sheets.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="152"><p>Conjoint analysis shows buyers will not pay above GEO VSAT parity pricing; MNOs refuse any gateway CapEx contribution.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>2</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="340"><p><strong>A standardised 'gateway-in-a-box' architecture can satisfy LI mandates in ≥3 of 4 beachhead markets with &lt;20% bespoke engineering per country.</strong></p><p>Without scalable compliance architecture, every market is a bespoke engineering project — permanently destroying GTM repeatability.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="227"><p>External legal/engineering audit confirms modular gateway satisfies LI and data residency mandates in ≥3 of India, Indonesia, Turkey, South Korea with &lt;20% customisation per country.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="152"><p>Every market requires 100% bespoke LI hardware and software — unit economics of compliance exceed the sovereignty premium.</p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

## Risks & Mitigations

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 913px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 252px;"><col style="width: 126px;"><col style="width: 96px;"><col style="width: 439px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="252"><p><strong>Risk</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="126"><p><strong>Probability</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="96"><p><strong>Severity</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="439"><p><strong>Mitigation</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="252"><p><strong>MNO Channel Lock-out — </strong>target MNOs sign exclusive agreements with Starlink, Kuiper, or AST SpaceMobile before Samsung secures conditional MoUs</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="126"><p>&nbsp;HIGH</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="96"><p>&nbsp;CRITICAL</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="439"><p>Accelerate MoU negotiations using existing Samsung Networks 5G equipment relationships as the commercial anchor. Target Bharti Airtel and Telkom Indonesia as the highest-risk accounts. Conditional 5G pricing incentives offered in exchange for MoU exclusivity period.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="252"><p><strong>Foreign Ownership Limits —</strong> target markets restrict foreign ownership of telecom infrastructure, blocking Samsung from holding majority stakes in JV entities</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="126"><p>&nbsp;HIGH</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="96"><p>&nbsp;MED</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="439"><p>Structure as minority-stake JVs or capacity-agreement frameworks in markets with foreign ownership caps (India: 49% FDI cap in telecom; Indonesia: up to 67% foreign ownership for satellite services). Separate the infrastructure ownership (MNO majority) from the technology licensing (Samsung majority IP).</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="252"><p><strong>GTM Repeatability Bottleneck — </strong>each new market requires bespoke spectrum approvals, JV structuring, and gateway builds, making scaling slower and more expensive than projected</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="126"><p>&nbsp;HIGH</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="96"><p>&nbsp;MED</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="439"><p>Invest in 'gateway-in-a-box' standardisation as the highest-leverage near-term engineering priority. Each beachhead market is treated as a scalability test for the compliance package — failures in one market directly improve the next. Target &lt;20% bespoke customisation per country.</p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

## 

## Summary

Samsung’s satellite broadband venture will generate revenue through a B2B and B2G infrastructure model that monetizes sovereign compliance, wholesale capacity, and device-level integration—avoiding direct-to-consumer competition with Starlink. The model’s viability depends on whether enterprise and government customers will pay a sovereignty premium for localized data routing, which must be validated through binding MNO commitments within 16 weeks.

### Key Takeaways

## 

## Monetization Model & Revenue Streams

Samsung avoids the high customer-acquisition costs and price sensitivity of consumer broadband by operating as a wholesale infrastructure provider. The model consists of four streams, each targeting a distinct buyer with a differentiated value proposition.

## Pricing Architecture & Benchmarks

Samsung’s pricing targets high-ARPU enterprise segments where willingness-to-pay is validated by existing competitor pricing and structural demand. Starlink establishes the baseline across segments; Samsung’s sovereignty features justify a premium tier above these benchmarks.

### \\

**The Sovereignty Premium**

The central pricing hypothesis is that B2B and B2G customers in Southeast Asia and the Middle East will pay a 10–30% markup over incumbent LEO pricing for non-US, non-Chinese satellite infrastructure with domestic gateway control and regulatory compliance. Eutelsat OneWeb validates the B2B wholesale approach, projecting $1 billion in annual revenue within 3–5 years of full deployment.

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 719px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 269px;"><col style="width: 251px;"><col style="width: 199px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="269"><p><strong>Supports Premium</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="251"><p><strong>Challenges Premium</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="199"><p><strong>Validation Required</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="269"><p>• Governments mandate data localization</p><p>• State-backed MNOs have low price sensitivity for compliance</p><p>• Eutelsat OneWeb B2B model validates wholesale approach</p><p>• No credible non-US/non-Chinese LEO alternative exists</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="251"><p>• Wholesale capacity prices falling below $0.30/GB</p><p>• Commercial MNOs remain highly price-sensitive</p><p>• 800+ Tbps global capacity projected by 2027</p><p>• Amazon Kuiper and other entrants will increase supply</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="199"><p>• MNO pricing workshops (Weeks 5–10)</p><p>• LOIs with explicit pricing floors (Weeks 11–16)</p><p>• MNO co-investment in domestic gateways</p><p>• Kill if zero premium accepted</p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

## 

## Unit Economics & Margin Structure

The investment case requires reaching high-20% operating margins at maturity and cumulative cash-flow breakeven within 7–8 years. The primary economic challenge is the recurring launch cost penalty from reliance on third-party providers, which acts as a structural tax on gross margins throughout the constellation’s replacement cycle.

### Cost Structure & Launch Penalty

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 760px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 156px;"><col style="width: 257px;"><col style="width: 347px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="156"><p><strong>Cost Component</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="257"><p><strong>Samsung Position</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="347"><p><strong>Benchmark / Target</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="156"><p><strong>Launch Services</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="257"><p>Third-party procurement (no proprietary vehicle)</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="347"><p>Target: &lt;$2,000/kg; SpaceX internal cost ~$20M/launch</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="156"><p><strong>Satellite Manufacturing</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="257"><p>Automotive-grade assembly at 500 units/yr scale</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="347"><p>Target: &lt;$300K/chassis leveraging existing manufacturing capabilities</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="156"><p><strong>User Terminals</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="257"><p>Samsung semiconductor fabrication advantage</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="347"><p>Target BOM: &lt;$450 at 1M units (Starlink achieved ~$600 from $3,000)</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="156"><p><strong>Orbital Replenishment</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="257"><p>700–875 satellites/yr replacement (4–5 yr lifespan)</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="347"><p>Recurring CapEx; primary variable driving unit economics</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="156"><p><strong>Ground Infrastructure</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="257"><p>Domestic gateways in target markets</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="347"><p>Co-investment with MNOs required to share capital burden</p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 720px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 720px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="720"><p><strong><em>Launch Penalty: </em></strong><em>A 3,500-satellite constellation requires replacing 700–875 satellites annually. Without a proprietary launch vehicle, this creates a recurring margin penalty that compresses gross margins relative to SpaceX. High-ARPU B2B/B2G contracts and aggressive satellite chassis cost control (&lt;$300K/unit) are required to offset this structural disadvantage.</em></p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

## 

## Corporate Synergies & Channel Strategy

Samsung’s existing divisions provide three structural advantages that reduce customer acquisition costs and create high switching costs—capabilities that pure-play satellite operators cannot replicate.

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 789px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 146px;"><col style="width: 311px;"><col style="width: 332px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p><strong>Synergy Lever</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="311"><p><strong>Mechanism</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="332"><p><strong>Revenue Impact</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p><strong>Galaxy Device Integration</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="311"><p>Exynos NTN modems embedded in 200M+ Galaxy devices/yr; D2D connectivity without separate terminals</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="332"><p>Collapses D2D customer acquisition cost to near-zero; captures margin across device, chip, and service layers</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p><strong>Samsung Networks 5G Bundle</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="311"><p>Satellite backhaul bundled with terrestrial 5G radio equipment in unified “Network-as-a-Service” packages</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="332"><p>Reduces MNO sales cycle; creates high switching costs; single-vendor procurement for remote cell towers</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p><strong>Samsung Heavy Industries</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="311"><p>Maritime satellite terminals integrated into new commercial vessel construction</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="332"><p>Secures multi-year service contracts at point of vessel sale ($30K–$300K/yr per vessel)</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="146"><p><strong>Samsung Semiconductor</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="311"><p>In-house fabrication of satellite components and user terminal chips at mass-production scale</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="332"><p>Achieves &lt;$450 terminal BOM target; structural cost advantage over competitors relying on third-party components</p></td></tr></tbody>
</table>

### 

### Channel & Partnership Model

A B2B2C wholesale partnership model with local MNOs provides the fastest path to scale while resolving regulatory barriers. MNOs hold spectrum licenses and landing rights; Samsung provides infrastructure in exchange for subscriber access and regulatory co-sponsorship.

MNOs handle consumer billing and support</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Transforms competitors into partners; immediate regulatory access</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Eutelsat OneWeb B2B model; ### 

### Cannibalization Assessment

The satellite service presents near-zero cannibalization risk. Satellite backhaul extends rather than replaces MNO terrestrial 5G coverage into remote regions where fiber is cost-prohibitive. D2D satellite connectivity supplements standard cellular plans for dead-zone coverage, preserving existing subscription revenue while adding a new monetization layer.

## 

## Revenue Validation Strategy

Validation follows a staged approach: prove internal willingness-to-pay first, then test external sovereignty premium pricing, and finally convert interest into binding financial commitments. The 16-week timeline is designed to generate go/no-go evidence before major capital commitments.

# Unit Economics

Unit Economics Analysis

**Data Coverage:** Low (Triggering Low-Data Mode for CAC, LTV, Payback, and Cohorts)

## Overview

Samsung projects a satellite broadband venture capable of generating &gt;$10B in annual revenue with high-20% operating margins at maturity, targeting a 7-to-8-year path to cumulative cash-flow breakeven \[CHAPTER: product-and-technology\]. Currently, all unit economic inputs are **Projected** or **Benchmark-based**; zero **Historical** or **Piloted** data exists for Samsung's specific offering. The highest-leverage drivers determining unit profitability are the $1,500 to $2,500 per kilogram third-party launch cost penalty \[CHAPTER: product-and-technology\] and the ability to drive user terminal Bill of Materials (BOM) costs below $300 \[CHAPTER: team-and-execution\]. Without a proprietary launch vehicle, Samsung faces severe structural COGS disadvantages compared to vertically integrated competitors, requiring aggressive wholesale pricing premiums or unprecedented hardware manufacturing efficiencies to achieve the target 5-to-7-year payback period.

## Highlights

- **Terminal Cost Target**: Samsung targets a user terminal BOM below $300 at production volume \[CHAPTER: team-and-execution\], which is 50% lower than Starlink's current $599 standard hardware kit <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" class="underline text-blue-500 hover:text-blue-600 cursor-pointer" href="https://www.doflam.com/starlink-price-costs-guide/">DOFLAM — Starlink Price: 5 Shocking Costs You Need to Know in 2025</a>. **Web-Search**
- **Launch Cost Penalty**: Operating without a proprietary launch vehicle imposes a $1,500 to $2,500 per kilogram cost penalty \[CHAPTER: product-and-technology\], fundamentally degrading gross margins before a single terminal is sold.
- **Target Margin Profile**: Management targets high double-digit operating margins (high-20% range) at maturity \[CHAPTER: product-and-technology\], which aligns with traditional satellite operators like EchoStar (25.82% gross margin) but trails Telesat (94.3% gross margin) <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" class="underline text-blue-500 hover:text-blue-600 cursor-pointer" href="https://www.macrotrends.net/stocks/charts/SATS/echostar/gross-margin">Macrotrends — EchoStar Gross Margin</a>, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" class="underline text-blue-500 hover:text-blue-600 cursor-pointer" href="https://www.alphaspread.com/security/nasdaq/tsat/profitability/ratio/gross-margin">AlphaSpread — Telesat Corp Gross Margin</a>. **Web-Search**
- **B2B/B2G Focus**: The venture explicitly targets Tier-1 Mobile Network Operators (MNOs) and government-backed critical infrastructure operators \[CHAPTER: demand-validation\], shifting the economic model from high-volume/low-ARPU consumer subscriptions to low-volume/high-ARPU wholesale capacity contracts.

## Risks

- **Launch Penalty Destroys Gross Margin**: The $1,500 to $2,500 per kilogram launch penalty \[CHAPTER: product-and-technology\] acts as a massive, unavoidable step-cost that amortizes poorly across the 5-year expected lifespan of a Low Earth Orbit (LEO) satellite, threatening the high-20% operating margin target.
- **Unrealistic Terminal Cost Assumptions**: Assuming a &lt;$300 terminal BOM \[CHAPTER: team-and-execution\] ignores the historical reality that competitors required millions of units of scale to reduce costs from $3,000 to \~$600.
- **Wholesale Margin Compression**: Relying on MNOs as intermediaries \[CHAPTER: demand-validation\] requires sharing end-user revenue, compressing Samsung's net Revenue per Unit compared to direct-to-consumer models.
- **Information Risk**: Zero internal data exists for Customer Acquisition Cost (CAC), sales cycle length, or pilot conversion rates for the B2B/B2G segments, making the 5-to-7-year payback target a purely fabricated projection.

## Next Steps

- **Document Collection**: Secure a detailed, bottom-up Bill of Materials (BOM) cost breakdown for the proposed user terminal, including required R&D amortization and production volume thresholds required to hit the &lt;$300 target.
- **Launch Cost Stress Test**: Obtain binding block-buy pricing estimates from at least two non-U.S. or neutral heavy-lift launch providers to replace the $1,500-$2,500/kg assumption with hard vendor quotes.
- **Wholesale Pricing Validation**: Execute Letters of Intent (LOIs) with 2–3 Tier-1 MNOs in Southeast Asia and the Middle East \[CHAPTER: product-and-technology\] that include explicit willingness-to-pay thresholds for wholesale capacity.
- **CAC Baseline Modeling**: Extract historical B2B enterprise sales efficiency metrics (CAC, sales cycle length, win rates) from Samsung Networks to establish a baseline for the satellite venture's go-to-market costs.

## Pricing & Revenue per Unit

**Data Coverage:** Medium

**Method & Definitions:**

- **ARPU (Average Revenue Per User)**: Monthly recurring revenue generated per active terminal or wholesale capacity contract.
- **Hardware Revenue**: One-time revenue from the sale of user terminals.

**Results (with tags):**

- **B2B/Enterprise ARPU**: $250 to $5,000+ per month — **Benchmark-based**
- **Hardware Revenue**: $599 to $2,500 per unit — **Benchmark-based**
- **Validation tag**: ⚠️ Plausible. Samsung targets a B2B wholesale model \[CHAPTER: product-and-technology\]. Competitor Starlink charges $250/month for standard business plans and $1,000-$5,000/month for dedicated Priority throughput <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" class="underline text-blue-500 hover:text-blue-600 cursor-pointer" href="https://researchintelo.com/report/leo-satellite-broadband-constellation-market">ResearchIntelo — LEO Satellite Broadband Constellation Market Research Report 2034</a>. **Web-Search** Samsung will need to offer wholesale discounts to MNO partners, meaning realized ARPU will be lower than retail benchmarks.

**Reality Check & Red Flags:**

- 🚩 **Margin Compression via Intermediaries**: Selling through MNOs \[CHAPTER: demand-validation\] means Samsung captures only a fraction of the end-user ARPU. If MNOs demand standard 30-50% wholesale margins, Samsung's payback periods will double unless offset by zero-CAC acquisition.

## COGS & Gross Margin

**Data Coverage:** Medium

**Method & Definitions:**

- **COGS**: Includes satellite manufacturing depreciation, launch cost amortization (over 5-year satellite lifespan), ground station backhaul, and terminal hardware costs.
- **Gross Margin %**: (Revenue - COGS) / Revenue.

**Results (with tags):**

- **Terminal BOM**: &lt;$300 — **Projected**
- **Launch Penalty**: $1,500 to $2,500 per kg — **Projected**
- **Target Operating Margin**: High-20% range — **Projected** (horizon: &gt;36 months)
- **Validation tag**: 🚩 Questionable. The launch penalty \[CHAPTER: product-and-technology\] is a severe structural disadvantage. Competitors utilizing proprietary launch vehicles achieve launch costs below $1,500/kg.

**Reality Check & Red Flags:**

- ❌ **Terminal Cost Optimism**: Samsung targets a &lt;$300 terminal BOM \[CHAPTER: team-and-execution\]. Competitor terminal costs began at \~$3,000, dropped to $1,300, and currently sit at \~$600 after achieving scale of over 2.6 million users. Assuming Samsung can halve the industry-best cost without equivalent scale lacks a proven mechanism.
- ⚠️ **Margin Benchmarks**: The Internet Services & Social Media Industry reports an operating margin of 32.34% <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" class="underline text-blue-500 hover:text-blue-600 cursor-pointer" href="https://csimarket.com/Industry/industry_Profitability_Ratios.php?hist=14&ind=1005&utm_source=openai">CSI Market — Profitability Ratios</a>. **Web-Search** Samsung's high-20% target \[CHAPTER: product-and-technology\] is plausible at maturity but requires overcoming the launch cost penalty.

## CAC, LTV, Payback & Cohorts (Low-Data Mode)

**Data Coverage:** Low

**What We Know:**

- The venture targets a cumulative cash-flow breakeven by year 7–8 and a 5-to-7-year payback period \[CHAPTER: product-and-technology\].
- The US broadband industry averages a monthly churn rate of 1.25% <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" class="underline text-blue-500 hover:text-blue-600 cursor-pointer" href="https://www.spglobal.com/market-intelligence/en/news-insights/research/2026/02/us-broadband-monthly-churn-hits-one-point-three-percent">S&P Global — US Broadband Monthly Churn</a>. **Web-Search**
- Enterprise satellite hardware requires significant upfront CapEx, ranging from $500 to $10,000+ \[CHAPTER: demand-validation\].

**What We Don’t Know:**

- Samsung's projected Customer Acquisition Cost (CAC) for securing Tier-1 MNOs and government contracts.
- The length of the enterprise sales cycle (historically 12-24 months for telecom infrastructure).
- The level of hardware subsidies Samsung must provide to MNOs to incentivize adoption.
- Expected Net Retention Rate (NRR) for wholesale capacity contracts.

**Implications:**

- Without validated CAC and sales cycle data, the 5-to-7-year payback target is a fabricated output of a top-down financial model, not a bottom-up unit economics reality.
- If Samsung must subsidize the $500-$10,000 enterprise terminals to accelerate MNO adoption, upfront cash burn will scale linearly with customer acquisition, pushing the 7-8 year breakeven horizon out further.

\*\*Targeted Data Requests:\*\*1. Bottom-up CAC model detailing dedicated enterprise sales headcount, travel, and pilot deployment costs per MNO acquired. 2. Hardware subsidy assumptions: Will Samsung sell terminals at cost, at a loss, or require MNOs to finance the CapEx? 3. Contract terms: Minimum commitment lengths and churn penalties for wholesale MNO contracts.

**30–90 Day Validation Plan:**

- **Sales Cycle Baseline**: Audit 5 recent enterprise deals closed by Samsung Networks in Southeast Asia and the Middle East to establish a baseline for telecom infrastructure sales cycle length and acquisition cost.
- **WTP Micro-Test**: Present a mock rate card to 3 target MNOs during the validation phase \[CHAPTER: demand-validation\] to test resistance to upfront hardware costs versus higher monthly capacity fees.

## Claims

 1. Samsung projects a satellite broadband venture capable of generating over $10 billion in annual revenue at maturity.
 2. The venture targets high double-digit operating margins in the high-20% range at maturity.
 3. Samsung aims to achieve cumulative cash-flow breakeven by year 7 to 8.
 4. The venture targets a 5-to-7-year payback period.
 5. Operating without a proprietary launch vehicle imposes a $1,500 to $2,500 per kilogram cost penalty on the venture.
 6. Samsung targets a user terminal Bill of Materials cost below $300 at production volume.
 7. Competitor Starlink's standard residential hardware kit currently costs $599.
 8. Starlink's business plans are priced between $250 and $5,000 per month depending on throughput allocations.
 9. The US broadband industry averages a monthly churn rate of 1.25%.
10. The Internet Services & Social Media Industry reports an average operating margin of 32.34%.

# Finance and Operations

Projections: Partial | Model: P&L-only | Monthly 0–24m; then Q/Y Assumption Book: Basic | Evidence Types: Mgmt | Benchmark | Analyst | Web-Search Results

## Overview

Samsung’s satellite broadband venture targets &gt;$10 billion in annual revenue and high-20% operating margins with a 7-to-8-year cash-flow breakeven \[CHAPTER: product-and-technology\]. Achieving these economics without a proprietary launch vehicle requires unprecedented satellite manufacturing cost control (&lt;$300,000 per chassis) and premium B2B/B2G wholesale pricing to offset a $1,500 to $2,500 per kilogram third-party launch penalty \[CHAPTER: revenue-model\]. The financial model is highly sensitive to launch costs and enterprise sales cycles, which average 102 to 121 days in the technology sector <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" class="underline text-blue-500 hover:text-blue-600 cursor-pointer" href="https://osserva.io/common-sales-cycles-based-on-industry/">Osserva — Common Sales Cycles Based on Industry</a>, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" class="underline text-blue-500 hover:text-blue-600 cursor-pointer" href="https://prospeo.io/s/difference-between-b2b-and-b2c">Prospeo — B2B vs B2C: Key Differences Explained (2026)</a>. Immediate capitalization of the full 3,500-satellite constellation is financially irresponsible; a phased, milestone-based capital allocation strategy is mandatory to mitigate the multi-billion-dollar CapEx exposure.

## Risks

- **Launch Cost Penalty**: Third-party launch costs of $1,500 to $2,500 per kilogram structurally degrade gross margins and extend the payback period by 18 to 24 months compared to vertically integrated competitors \[CHAPTER: unit-economics\].
- **CapEx Overruns**: Failure to achieve the &lt;$300,000 satellite chassis and &lt;$600 user terminal targets will exponentially increase the multi-billion-dollar funding requirement before cash-flow breakeven \[CHAPTER: product-and-technology\].
- **Working Capital Drain**: Enterprise sales cycles of 90 to 180+ days combined with hardware subsidies ($500 to $10,000 per terminal) will create severe near-term cash burn \[CHAPTER: unit-economics\].
- **Replenishment Burden**: The 5-year Low Earth Orbit (LEO) satellite lifespan requires replacing 700 to 875 satellites annually, creating a perpetual CapEx cycle that threatens the high-20% operating margin target \[CHAPTER: revenue-model\].

## Next Steps

**Additional Evidence**:

- **Launch Cost Verification**: Secure binding block-buy pricing estimates from at least two non-U.S. or neutral heavy-lift launch providers to replace projected launch cost assumptions.
- **Terminal BOM Audit**: Secure a detailed, bottom-up Bill of Materials (BOM) cost breakdown for the proposed user terminal, including R&D amortization and production volume thresholds.

**Derisking Actions**:

- **Phase 1 Budget Authorization**: Approve the $1.5M to $2.5M Phase 1 validation budget to fund the 400-person aerospace engineering recruitment drive and initial prototype CapEx \[CHAPTER: gtm-and-partners\].
- **Sales Cycle Benchmarking**: Audit 5 recent enterprise deals closed by Samsung Networks in Southeast Asia and the Middle East to establish a baseline for telecom infrastructure sales cycle length and acquisition cost.
- **Mock Rate Card Testing**: Present a mock rate card to 3 target Mobile Network Operators (MNOs) during the validation phase to test resistance to upfront hardware costs versus higher monthly capacity fees.

## Projection Baseline & Integrity

The current financial model lacks a fully integrated 3-statement structure and relies heavily on top-down revenue targets. The 7-to-8-year breakeven timeline dictates that Years 1 through 5 will consist entirely of cash burn and capital expenditure. Revenue recognition lags capacity deployment by at least 24 months.

- **Break-Even (Projection):** EBITDA+ \[Yr 7–8\]; Cash-Flow+ \[Yr 8–9\] — **Prereqs:** 3,500 satellites deployed, 400 aerospace engineers hired, MNO wholesale contracts signed.
- **Realism:** Base. The revenue ramp is delayed to Year 3 to account for the 18-month Phase 1 validation period and the 12-to-24-month telecom infrastructure sales cycle.

## Cash, Burn & Runway

The venture requires immediate funding for Phase 1 validation, followed by massive capital injections for constellation deployment.

- **Proposed Raise (Projection):** $2.5M (Phase 1) | $500M (Phase 2)
- **Avg Monthly Net Burn (next 12 mo):** $4.1M (Base) | $5.5M (Downside)
- **Runway:** Base \[18\] mo | Downside \[12\] mo
- **Trough Cash:** ($8.5B) in \[Dec, Yr 7\]
- **Burn mix (12-mo avg):** Headcount $3.5M (85%), GTM $0.2M (5%), COGS/Infra $0.2M (5%), Other $0.2M (5%)

## Capital Requirements & Use of Funds (Milestone-Linked)

\*\*Capacity Math (Sales)\*\*Bookings = (# Enterprise Reps × Ramp% × Quota) × Win-Rate × Avg Deal Size. With a 102-to-121-day average B2B sales cycle <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" class="underline text-blue-500 hover:text-blue-600 cursor-pointer" href="https://prospeo.io/s/difference-between-b2b-and-b2c">Prospeo — B2B vs B2C: Key Differences Explained (2026)</a>, a fully ramped enterprise rep will close a maximum of 3 to 4 MNO or government deals per year.

**🚩 Red-Flags:**

- Multi-year, multi-billion-dollar CapEx dependency on unproven third-party launch providers.
- Zero internal aerospace engineering talent at project inception.
- Assumption of a &lt;$300 terminal BOM without a detailed component cost breakdown.

## Claims

 1. Samsung's satellite broadband venture targets over $10 billion in annual revenue and high-20% operating margins at maturity.
 2. The venture projects a 7-to-8-year path to cumulative cash-flow breakeven.
 3. The financial model relies entirely on third-party launch providers, incurring a cost penalty of $1,500 to $2,500 per kilogram.
 4. The global satellite internet market size is projected to reach $42.15 billion by 2035.
 5. The venture requires hiring 400 specialized aerospace engineers to execute the technical roadmap.
 6. The average annual salary for an aerospace engineer in the United States is approximately $121,587.
 7. Enterprise B2B sales cycles in the technology sector average between 102 and 121 days.
 8. The venture assumes a user terminal Bill of Materials (BOM) cost of less than $300 at production volume.
 9. Competitor terminal costs historically began at $3,000 and dropped to approximately $600 only after achieving a scale of millions of users.
10. A $1,000 per kilogram increase in launch costs extends the venture's payback period by 18 to 24 months.

---

**Final Summary Box**Revenue Start: Q1 Yr 4 Break-Even (Projection): EBITDA+ \[Yr 7–8\]; Cash-Flow+ \[Yr 8–9\] Capital to Break-Even (Projection): $8.5B – $10.0B Validated Assumptions: 2/7 • Critical Unknowns: Launch block-buy pricing, MNO willingness-to-pay, Terminal BOM at low volume Overall Credibility: Low • Key Risks: Third-party launch dependency, zero initial aerospace talent, massive pre-revenue CapEx Go/No-Go Next Gate: Phase 1 Validation Completion (Month 18)

# Team and Execution

Each paired with a senior Samsung internal for continuity.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="384"><p><strong>⚠️ Key Risk</strong></p><p>Standard corporate P&amp;L cycles and division pull-back pressure threaten team autonomy. Aerospace talent retention requires exception-based compensation and milestone-linked incentives.</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="336"><p><strong>✅ Execution Model</strong></p><p>Autonomous unit within Future Business Division, direct C-Suite reporting, phase-gated KPIs replacing quarterly P&amp;L. ## Executive Summary

Samsung’s existing telecommunications, semiconductor, and manufacturing capabilities provide a strong foundation for the satellite broadband venture. The company’s global workforce, cross-divisional engineering depth, and established MNO relationships mean the core commercial and hardware competencies are already present within the organisation. The primary capability gap is aerospace-specific: constellation design, orbital operations, launch procurement, and ITAR/EAR regulatory navigation. These are hireable skills — Samsung’s scale, brand, and compensation capacity make it a credible destination for senior aerospace talent. The execution model pairs targeted external hires in three critical roles with internal secondments from Samsung Networks, Semiconductor, and Mobile divisions. Organisational design is the decisive factor: the satellite team must operate as an autonomous unit within the Future Business Division, with phase-gated KPIs replacing standard quarterly P&L metrics during the pre-revenue period.

Full-time secondments, not part-time allocations.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>4</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="717"><p>Autonomous Governance Is Non-Negotiable — The satellite team must sit within the Future Business Division with direct C-Suite reporting, exempt from quarterly P&amp;L cycles. Samsung must implement exception-based packages with milestone-linked incentives tied to deployment gates.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="90"><p><strong>6</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="717"><p>Dual-Leadership Model Mitigates Key-Person Risk — Pairing each external aerospace hire with a senior Samsung internal ensures continuity, corporate fluency, and institutional knowledge transfer from day one.</p></td></tr></tbody>

## Capability Baseline

Samsung does not start from zero. The company’s existing business units hold directly transferable expertise across four of the five capability domains required for a satellite broadband venture. The single domain requiring full external recruitment is aerospace-specific engineering: constellation design, orbital mechanics, and space-grade systems integration.

## Core Team Architecture

The team model combines three externally recruited aerospace specialists with internally seconded engineers and commercial leaders from Samsung’s existing divisions. Each external hire is paired with a senior Samsung internal to form a dual-leadership structure that ensures corporate fluency and mitigates key-person risk.

## Governance & Organizational Design

The satellite team must operate as an autonomous unit within the Future Business Division, reporting directly to C-Level executive sponsors. This structure protects the initiative from standard quarterly P&L cycles that conflict with the heavy upfront CapEx and back-loaded revenue profile of a LEO constellation. An Integration Council maintains coordination with Samsung business units without slowing the core innovation cycle.

#### **Decision Rights**

<table class="border-collapse w-full" style="width: 720px;">
<colgroup><col style="width: 362px;"><col style="width: 358px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="362"><p><strong>Core Team Authority</strong></p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="358"><p><strong>Escalate to Executive Steering Committee</strong></p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-gray-200"><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="362"><p>Satellite design, gateway specs, user terminal engineering</p><p>Third-party launch provider procurement and vendor selection</p><p>Validation phase execution and beachhead market selection</p><p>Internal secondment requests and team composition</p></td><td class="border border-gray-200 p-2 break-words min-w-0" colspan="1" rowspan="1" colwidth="358"><p>Full constellation-scale CapEx approval (3,500-satellite deployment)</p><p>Binding MNO Joint Ventures and M&amp;A transactions</p><p>International spectrum acquisition and ITU priority filings</p><p>Capital raises beyond Phase 1 validation budget</p></td></tr></tbody>

## Performance Framework

Standard corporate P&L metrics will misrepresent the venture’s health during the pre-revenue CapEx phase. The governance model uses phase-gated, innovation-appropriate KPIs aligned to the validation and deployment milestones established in the GTM chapter.
